\chapter{Artificial Intelligence (AI)}\label{artificial-intelligence-ai}

\section{Introduction}\label{introduction}

\textbf{Why} do we bother with \textbf{this topic} at all?

For a \textbf{simple reason}: The release of ChatGPT in late 2022
has not only sparked a new wave of interest in AI,
but has fundamentally influenced

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  how we write text,
\item
  code,
\item
  how we search the internet,
\item
  create images and
\item
  solve problems.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Another reason} could be given. As far as I am aware,
\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_network_(machine_learning)}{\textbf{neural networks}}
(an important part of AI, more on that topic later) are a
true \textbf{extension of our regression models}.
Hence, the whole regression framework
can be subsumed under the umbrella of AI.

One thing should be stated at the beginning. The field and applications are moving
so fast that it is impossible to keep up with the latest developments.
So, please forgive me if some of the information is outdated or not up to date.

The standard textbook on AI is Russell and Norvig's
\href{hhttps://people.engr.tamu.edu/guni/csce625/slides/AI.pdf}{Artificial Intelligence: A Modern Approach}
(3rd edition, 2020), currently in its 4th edition.

Other good (non-technical) books on the topic are:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Melanie Mitchell's \href{https://melaniemitchell.me/aibook/}{Artificial Intelligence: A Guide for Thinking Humans}
\item
  Gary Marcus's \href{Rebooting\%20Ai:\%20Building\%20Artificial\%20Intelligence\%20We\%20Can\%20Trust}{Rebooting AI: Building Artificial Intelligence We Can Trust}
\item
  Stuart Russell's \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Compatible}{Human Compatible: Artificial Intelligence and the Problem of Control}
\item
  Max Tegmark's \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_3.0}{Life 3.0: Being Human in the Age of Artificial Intelligence}
\end{itemize}

The first two authors seem to be less afraid that AI could take over the world,
while the latter two authors are more concerned about the implications of AI on society and humanity.

\subsection{A short history of AI}\label{a-short-history-of-ai}

Artificial Intelligence (AI) refers to the design and development of systems
that can perform tasks typically requiring human intelligence---such as reasoning,
learning, perception, problem-solving, and natural language understanding.

The ambition to build machines that mimic or surpass human cognitive abilities
is not new and can be traced back to myths, philosophical debates, and early
mechanical automata.

The formal \textbf{birth of AI as a field} is generally dated to \textbf{1956},
when John McCarthy, Marvin Minsky, Claude Shannon, and Nathaniel Rochester
organized the \emph{Dartmouth Summer Research Project on Artificial Intelligence}.
This conference was the first to coin the term ``artificial intelligence'' and
laid the foundation for decades of exploration into machine cognition.

However, the intellectual roots of AI go much further back. \textbf{Alan Turing},
in his seminal 1950 paper \href{https://academic.oup.com/mind/article-abstract/LIX/236/433/986238?redirectedFrom=fulltext}{\emph{``Computing Machinery and Intelligence''}},
asked the
famous question: ``Can machines think?'' He proposed the \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turing_test}{\textbf{Turing Test}}
as a way to evaluate a machine's ability to exhibit intelligent behavior
indistinguishable from that of a human. Turing's work established a
theoretical framework that deeply influenced how researchers would later
define machine intelligence.

In the early decades following Dartmouth, AI research oscillated between
periods of optimism and disappointment.
The \textbf{1950s--1970s} were marked by the development of symbolic AI and
rule-based systems. Programs like ELIZA (a simple natural language processor)
and SHRDLU (which operated in a limited ``blocks world'') showcased early
attempts at mimicking human-like reasoning. \textbf{Researchers believed that general
AI} (AGI) was just around the corner.

This optimism faded in the \textbf{1970s and 1980s}, leading to what became known
as the \emph{``AI winter''}---a period characterized by reduced funding and slowed
progress due to unmet expectations. The limitations of symbolic logic
in handling uncertainty and perception-heavy tasks became clear.
However, during this time, important subfields like \textbf{expert systems}
and \textbf{machine learning} began to evolve quietly.

AI entered a new phase in the late 1990s and early 2000s, buoyed by greater
computational power, larger datasets, and improved algorithms.
Milestones such as
\textbf{IBM's Deep Blue defeating chess champion Garry Kasparov in 1997},
and later, \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KF6sLCeBj0s&ab_channel=BBCNews}{\textbf{Watson winning \emph{Jeopardy!} in 2011}},
demonstrated tangible progress.

The last decade, however, marked a major \textbf{paradigm shift}, especially with
the rise of \textbf{deep learning}, a form of machine learning using artificial
neural networks. In 2012, a convolutional neural network (AlexNet) won the
\href{https://www.image-net.org/challenges/LSVRC/}{ImageNet} competition,
drastically improving image recognition benchmarks.
This breakthrough ignited an AI renaissance that led to the development
of powerful models such as \textbf{GPT (by OpenAI)} and \textbf{BERT (by Google)}---transforming
fields like natural language processing, computer vision, and robotics.

Today, AI spans everything from autonomous vehicles to medical diagnostics,
and the field is increasingly focused not just on capability,
but on \textbf{safety, alignment, interpretability}, and \textbf{ethics}.
While we are still far from artificial general intelligence (AGI),
the rapid progress in AI systems is reshaping industries, science, and society.

In the meantime, AlphaGo, a program developed by Google DeepMind,
\href{https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/AlphaGo_gegen_Lee_Sedol}{defeated}
the world champion Go player Lee Sedol in 2016. As with chess,
many people thought that Go would be too complex for a computer to master.

\href{https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/chemistry/2024/hassabis/facts/}{Demis Hassabis},
the founder of DeepMind,
won the Nobel Prize in chemistry 2024 for his work on AlphaFold2, which
solved the \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2443096/\#:~:text=The\%20protein\%20folding\%20problem\%20is,first\%20atomic\%2Dresolution\%20protein\%20structures.}{protein folding problem}.

\subsection{ChatGPT}\label{chatgpt}

The public release of \href{https://openai.com/index/chatgpt/}{ChatGPT}
(\textbf{G}eneralized \textbf{P}retrained \textbf{T}ransformer) in November 2022, based on OpenAI's GPT-3.5,
marked a watershed moment in the history of artificial intelligence.
While \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_language_model}{\textbf{large language models (LLMs)}}
had already impressed researchers,
ChatGPT was the first to make them widely accessible through a conversational interface.
Suddenly, millions of people could ask questions,
get code suggestions, summarize documents, or even write poetry---all through natural dialogue.

By early 2023, ChatGPT had become a global phenomenon, reaching 100 million users within two months,
making it the \href{https://cdn.buttercms.com/output=f:webp/lgJEtn9sTLu1AflcBKDy}{fastest-growing consumer application in history}.
It changed the public perception
of AI from futuristic and experimental to practical and omnipresent.

In March 2023, OpenAI released \href{https://arxiv.org/pdf/2303.08774}{\textbf{GPT-4}},
a much improved model in terms of reasoning, factual accuracy,
and multilingual capabilities. GPT-4 also \textbf{introduced image input}
and began to bridge the gap toward \textbf{multimodal AI} (not limited to text input).

Here is an example of an image described by GPT-4o (a more advanced version of GPT-4):
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{bookdown-demo_files/images/GPT_4_image_description.png}}
(Created with ChatGPT macOS app, 21.5.24)

At the time, the perfectly worded image description from GPT felt like magic.
One way to achieve this \emph{image captioning} is explained by Mike Pound \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KcSXcpluDe4&t=866s&ab_channel=Computerphile}{here}.
The \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transformer_(deep_learning_architecture)}{transformer} architecture (a so-called vision transformer, ViT) is involved
in this process, which is a \textbf{key component} of many modern AI systems.
We will not go into details here. Interestingly, the transformer architecture
was introduced by researchers at Google in 2017 in a paper called
\href{https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2017/file/3f5ee243547dee91fbd053c1c4a845aa-Paper.pdf}{Attention is All You Need},
which was cited\ldots{}

\begin{Shaded}

\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(stringr)}

\NormalTok{url }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \StringTok{"https://scholar.google.com/scholar?hl=en\&q=attention+is+all+you+need"}
\NormalTok{page }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_html}\NormalTok{(url)}

\begin{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

As LLMs matured, the focus shifted (also) to \textbf{autonomous agents}.
Systems like \href{https://agpt.co/}{AutoGPT},
\href{https://babyagi.org/}{BabyAGI} (AGI stands for Artificial General Intelligence),
and later OpenAI's own GPTs (custom bots) experimented with
giving models access to tools (e.g., browsers, calculators, APIs), memory, and goals.
One failed attempt to sell AI agents as an end-user product was
the \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ddTV12hErTc&ab_channel=MarquesBrownlee}{Rabbit R1}.

For people in the field, it was clear the technology was not ready for this type of application yet.
I am not sure how it has progressed in the meantime, but a year ago the practical use of these
agents was still limited.
Google announced ``Agent Mode'' \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LxvErFkBXPk&ab_channel=Google}{recently}.
There have been promises of AI agents performing tasks
(like calling a restaurant to make an appointment) autonomously \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D5VN56jQMWM&ab_channel=JeffGrubb\%27sGameMess}{before}.

The vision is no longer just chat, but delegating tasks: ``book my flights,'' ``analyze this dataset,'' or
``write a full report with citations.''
This period also saw growing demand for interpretability, trustworthiness, and alignment.
Questions about hallucinations, bias, and safety became central---not only for researchers,
but for businesses and governments. RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) became a
standard technique to make models more helpful and aligned with human intent.

Meanwhile, open-source efforts accelerated. Meta released LLaMA, and others followed with
models like Falcon, Mistral, and Mixtral. Although initially less powerful, these models
enabled broader research, customization, and deployment, especially in privacy-sensitive environments.

\section{Capabilities of LLMs}\label{capabilities-of-llms}

A model like GPT-4 can perform a wide range of tasks, including:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Text Generation}: Writing essays, articles, or creative content.
  \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LPZh9BOjkQs&t=45s&ab_channel=3Blue1Brown}{\emph{Very} simplified},
  this is done by \textbf{predicting the next word} in a sequence based on
  the context of the previous words. GPT-4 was trained using
  \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinforcement_learning_from_human_feedback}{Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF)}.
  More on reinforcement learning later.
  But the \emph{Human Feedback} means that humans rated the quality of the generated text.
\item
  \textbf{Image generation}: Creating \href{https://openai.com/index/introducing-4o-image-generation/}{images}
  from text prompts. This has improved during the last 1-2 years.
  I assume that OpenAI uses \emph{very} advanced \textbf{diffusion models} for this task, the basic principles
  are rather graspable though
  (\href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1CIpzeNxIhU&t=866s&ab_channel=Computerphile}{1},
  \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1pgiu--4W3I&t=1273s&ab_channel=DepthFirst}{2}).

  Example picture generated by GPT-4o:
  \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{bookdown-demo_files/images/GPT4_me.png}}
\item
  \textbf{Question Answering}: Providing answers to factual questions or summarizing information.
  Until this day, LLMs sometimes hallucinate, i.e., they make up facts or
  provide incorrect information with a high degree of confidence.
  They are trained on a large corpus of text to produce reasonable sounding answers.
\item
  \textbf{Translation}: Translating text between multiple languages. This works rather excellent
  and brings back memories from my English teacher who told me that
  language will never be mastered by a computer.
\item
  \textbf{Code Generation}: Writing and debugging code in various programming languages.
\item
  \textbf{Image Understanding}: Analyzing and describing images (in multimodal versions).
\item
  \textbf{Conversational Agents}: Engaging in natural language conversations,
  simulating human-like dialogue.
\end{itemize}

Sébastien Bubeck published a paper in 2023 titled\\
\href{https://arxiv.org/pdf/2303.12712}{``Sparks of Artificial General Intelligence:
Early Experiments with GPT-4''},
which explores the capabilities of GPT-4 in depth.
As in other technical reports of commercial models,
the so-called model weight (more on that later) are not disclosed.
